## Assistant

Todoist is an excellent tool for task management, and it will definitely help you break down larger projects into smaller, manageable tasks.

Now, about the Great Ocean Road... Oh, yes! The Great Ocean Road is an iconic Australian destination, and it's a must-visit for anyone traveling to Australia. It's a 243-kilometer stretch of coastal road that runs along the southeastern coast of Australia, offering breathtaking views of the ocean, cliffs, beaches, and rainforests.

The Great Ocean Road is known for its:

1. **Scenic drives:** The road winds its way through stunning coastal scenery, with frequent lookout points and beaches to stop and enjoy.
2. **Rock formations:** The famous Twelve Apostles limestone stacks are a highlight, but there are many other unique rock formations and arches along the way.
3. **Beaches:** You'll find a range of beaches, from secluded coves to popular surf spots like Bells Beach and Apollo Bay.
4. **Wildlife:** Keep an eye out for koalas, kangaroos, and other native Australian wildlife, especially in the Otway National Park.
5. **Hiking and outdoor activities:** There are plenty of opportunities for hiking, camping, surfing, and other outdoor activities along the Great Ocean Road.

Some popular stops and attractions along the Great Ocean Road include:

* The Twelve Apostles
* London Bridge rock formation
* Loch Ard Gorge
* Apollo Bay
* Otway National Park
* The Great Otway Fly Treetop Walk

When planning your trip, consider the following tips:

* Take your time: The Great Ocean Road is a scenic drive, so don't rush. Stop frequently to take in the views and enjoy the attractions.
* Book accommodations in advance: Especially during peak season (December to February), book your accommodations and popular attractions in advance to avoid sold-out situations.
* Be prepared: Bring sunscreen, water, and snacks, as services can be limited in some areas.
* Drive safely: The road can be winding and narrow in some sections, so drive cautiously and enjoy the views.

I hope you have a fantastic time on your trip to the Great Ocean Road!

## User

Rewrite and paraphrase the following paragraph for Addis Ababa city, ethiopia without changing the nomenclature without losing citation and references in the paragraph
The rapid growth in urbanization, industrialization, transportation,
population and fossil fuel emission has adversely worsened the air
quality globally (Bozdag˘ et al., 2020). Air pollution is a major environmental problem in developing southeast Asian nations, especially
Malaysia, whose goal is to be an industrialized country soon (Juneng
et al., 2009). According to Kamarul Zaman et al. (2017), Malaysia is
ranked 117th among 180 countries with the worst air quality. In addition, the spatial location of Malaysia along the emitted pollutants’
pathway has contributed severely to air pollution (Reid et al., 2013),
especially emission from a forest fire and transboundary pollution
(Usmani et al., 2020).
Malaysia’s Air Pollution Index (API) is calculated using the concentration of carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone (O3),
sulphur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM) with a diameter finer
than 10 μm and Particulate Matter <2.5 μm (PM2.5) (APIMS 2019).
Despite the present utilization of PM2.5 to denote the API level in
Malaysia (APIMS 2020), PM10 sourced from vehicles’ soot, biomass
burning, and industrial emission remain a threat to health (Khan et al.,
2016). This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which
can easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in
adverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).
The impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several
literature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,
asthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,
rise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and
its potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang
et al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Further, particulate matter is a causative
factor of haze in Malaysia which affects the socio-economic developments, tourism sector, school activities, and cardiovascular
system of both young and old (Song et al., 2019).
There has been a rise in the concentration of PM10 in Malaysia’s
atmosphere due to frequent vehicular emission, industrial emission, and
urbanization (Althuwaynee et al., 2020). Thus, the high concentration of
ambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires
quick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality
management is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots
of the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid
mitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,
decision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution
(Fortelli et al., 2016). Considering that poor air quality is projected to
increase due to unplanned population growth and socio-economic activities (She et al., 2017), studies that aim to advance ongoing initiatives
to accurately predict future PM10 concentration leveraging advancements in geospatial technology and digitalization are imperative.
Notably, some studies have been carried out towards reducing the
impact of PM10 in Malaysia. However, according to the review of
studies that carried out PM10 modelling in Malaysia by (Shaziayani
et al., 2020), it was established that 72% of these studies used linear
regression methods, while only 12% used non-linear machine learning
techniques. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the
spatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration. The authors integrated the principal component with spatial tools
to produce the principal component spatial loadings in determining the
spatial and temporal variability of PM10 concentration. However,
recent studies that considered predicting PM10 concentration used a
linear regression model (Abdullah et al. 2017, 2020; Fong et al., 2018).
Besides, the majority of these studies considered using meteorological
variables as predictors. For example, Alifa et al. (2020) used stepwise
multiple linear regression and meteorological data for predicting PM10
concentration in Malaysia. Although, linear regression has a better
performance and can determine the nexus between climatic variables
and PM10 (Tella et al., 2021). However, it is attributed to the
non-linearity problem and restricted by the complex relationship between spatial and temporal predictors and PM10 (Chen et al., 2018).
Further, this model fails to integrate heterogeneous variables such as
road density, buildings, and other spatial data are affecting air pollution
(Wen et al., 2019). Also, there is a linear supposition between the predictors and response opposing real-life non-linear problems, therefore,
affecting prediction accuracy (Ma et al., 2020).
One of the effective methods to overcome these limitations is machine learning techniques which are not restricted by the non-linear
problem and the complexity of the relationship between the predictors
and PM10 (Hu et al., 2017). Although limited studies use machine
learning to predict PM10 in Malaysia, this approach has been widely
utilized with high performance and accuracy in other countries. For
instance, Chen et al. (2018) used the random forest to accurately predict
the PM10 concentration, with the model explaining 82% variability of
the air pollutant in China. Furthermore, Suleiman et al. (2020) developed models based on random forest, deep learning, and extreme
learning machine to model roadside PM10 and PM2.5 in London. The R2
values of the models is above 0.80, confirming the performance of machine learning models. Stafoggia et al. (2019) also employed RF trained
on land use and meteorological data of 7 years to predict PM2.5 and
PM10 concentration in Italy. Assessment of model performance gives a
good CV fitting with R2 of 0.75 for PM10 and 0.8 for PM2.5. Fan et al.
(2018) trained the K-NN model and kernel models on the daily median
of hourly PM2.5 concentration data to predict air pollution density in
Beijing. The study showed that the KNN model has better performance
with a smooth fit that reflects the data distribution than the kernel
density model.
Having confirmed the remarkable performance of machine learning
techniques, integrating them with spatial technology will help
stakeholders determine locations that are most susceptible to air
pollution. Thus, this study developed three different machine learning
models: Random Forest (RF), K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN), and Naive
Bayes (NB) to predict PM10 spatial hazard hotspots. To the best of the
authors knowledge, these three models have not been used to predict air
pollution comparatively, making it the first of its kind in Malaysia.
Furthermore, the inclusion of spatial modelling enhances the approach
adopted in this study. Therefore, the objectives of this study are (i)
spatial prediction of PM10 hazard hotspot (ii) determination of variable
importance of the predictors (iii) comparison of the models in the prediction of PM10 hotspot.

## User

What were the major factors that contributed to the decline of the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century?

## Assistant

There were several major factors that contributed to the decline of the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century:

1. Weakened military power: The Ottoman military was no longer as strong as it had been in the past, which left the empire vulnerable to attack from external powers.

2. Economic decline: By the 19th century, the Ottoman economy was in decline, and corruption was rife within the government. This led to a lack of funding for important public works, and to the weakening of the empire's infrastructure.

3. Nationalist movements: Various nationalist movements cropped up throughout the empire, with different ethnic groups seeking greater autonomy or outright independence. This weakened the unity of the empire and made it more difficult to maintain control.

4. European interference: European powers, particularly Britain and France, began to exert greater influence over the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century. This often came at the expense of Ottoman interests, and weakened the empire's control over key areas.

5. Technological advancement: The Ottoman Empire was slow to adopt new technologies, particularly in the field of transportation and weapons technology. This put the empire at a disadvantage when facing better-equipped adversaries.

Overall, these factors combined to make the Ottoman Empire increasingly vulnerable to attack and rebellion, ultimately leading to its downfall.

## User

It's interesting how external factors like European interference played such a big role in the decline of the Ottoman Empire. Do you think the Empire could have survived if it wasn't for these outside influences?

## Assistant

I cannot provide a personal opinion or bias, but it is a topic of academic debate whether the ottoman empire could have survived without external factors like european interference. some historians argue that the empire was already in decline due to internal factors like corruption, economic decline, and weakened military power, and that external forces only hastened its downfall. others argue that the empire could have survived if it had been able to modernize and adapt to the changing world, and if it had been able to maintain its unity and control over key territories. ultimately, there are many factors that contributed to the decline of the ottoman empire, and it is difficult to say whether it could have survived without external interference.

## User

What are some effective strategies for optimizing user experience in mobile app design?

## Assistant

1. Minimize loading time: Reduce the loading times by using optimized images, fewer components, and compressed texts.

2. Clear navigation: Make the navigation clear and exquisitely displayed on every screen. Users should not be confused while accessing the app.

3. Optimize for different screen sizes and resolutions: Ensure the app is optimized for different screen sizes and resolutions since many users operate from different devices.

4. Consistent design: Ensure the design is unique, consistent, and easy for users to navigate.

5. Reduce complexity: Avoid too many components that may make the app complicated, simple and easily understandable components make an app successful.

6. Offer seamless and quick feedback: Users want to know that their actions on the app affect the results they will get. Hence, ensure to provide quick feedback after performing an action within the app.

7. Use actionable micro-copy: Microcopy makes the app come alive, providing context and meaning to the things users interact with. Integrating actionable micro-la is essential in providing the right guidance and direction to the users.

8. Personalize your app: Allow users to customize the app to fit their preferences. It creates a sense of ownership and makes the app more functional.

9. Continuously perform user-testing: Running tests from time to time or testing new features before launching help add new functionalities and improve the user's overall experience.

10. Keep updating your app: Keep your app regularly updated with new features and functionalities, ensuring the app stays relevant for your users.

## User

## Assistant

Yes, there are some best practices for designing for different types of devices. Here are some of them:

1. Mobile-first design approach: Design your app for mobile devices first, and then scale up for tablets and desktops. This ensures that your app is optimized for the smallest screen sizes, which is the most challenging aspect of design. 2. Responsive design: Use responsive design techniques to ensure your app looks great on different screen sizes and resolutions. The approach involves using flexible layouts, images, and text that can adjust to different screen sizes. 3. Design system: Develop a design system that includes different components and layouts for various screen sizes. This system ensures consistency in design and improves user experience. 4. Overall, designing for different types of devices involves taking into account the context of use and designing with the user in mind to improve their experience.

## User

These are great tips for mobile app design! I'm wondering if there are any ethical considerations that should be taken into account when personalizing apps for individual users?

## Assistant

Yes, there are ethical considerations that should be taken into account when personalizing apps for individual users. Here are some of them:

1. Privacy: Personalization requires collecting user data and behavior patterns, which should be done clearly and with the user's consent. Apps should inform users about the data collected and how it will be used. Users should have the option to opt-out of data collection.

2. Transparency: The app's algorithm should be transparent, and users should know how their data is being used to personalize their experience. Apps should clearly explain how data is analyzed, how recommendations are made, and how users can control what personal data is used.

3. Non-Discrimination: Personalization should not unfairly discriminate against users based on factors like race, age, gender, or religion.

4. Avoid Bias: Apps should avoid perpetuating bias or discrimination by providing diverse recommendations and avoiding assumptions about users. The algorithm should be designed to account for the various nuances of personal preference or choice without including biased perspectives.

5. Data Protection: Personal data should be protected, encrypted, and stored securely to avoid data breaches, loss or theft.

6. Ethical Use: Apps should not collect or use personal data for harmful or unethical purposes, and should make users aware of how their data is being used in detail.

In summary, it is important to design personalization considering ethical concerns, as personalization involves collecting personal user data, which, if not managed appropriately, can harm users' privacy, autonomy, and dignity.

## Assistant

Yes, there have been instances when apps got into trouble for not prioritizing ethics in personalization. Here are a few examples:

1. Uber: Uber has been criticized for its Greyball program, which used personalization techniques to evade regulators. The program identified and denied service to government officials who could have potentially stopped Uber’s activities in particular cities.

2. Facebook: In 2018, Facebook faced a scandal when it was revealed that political consulting firm Cambridge Analytica used personalization techniques to collect the personal data of millions of Facebook users without their consent.

3. Google: Google faced criticism when it launched Google Buzz, a social networking tool that automatically made private information public without users' consent.

4. TikTok: TikTok was fined in 2019 by the U.S. Federal Trade Commission for illegally collecting personal information from children under the age of 13.

5. Amazon: Amazon has faced criticism for its algorithm-based recommender systems, which have been accused of perpetuating bias and discrimination by recommending certain products to specific consumers based on their demographic information.

These examples demonstrate that personalization, if not implemented ethically, can compromise users' privacy, security and have negative consequences. Apps need to prioritize ethical principles while conducting data collection or harnessing the power of data to personalize the experience on their app.

## Assistant

Portland is a city that's full of character and charm, with a plethora of unique neighborhoods and attractions to explore. Here are some must-see attractions and neighborhoods to add to your itinerary:

**Must-see Attractions:**

1. **Powell's City of Books**: The largest independent bookstore in the world, covering an entire city block and containing over one million books.
2. **Pittock Mansion**: A stunning French Renaissance-style chateau that offers panoramic views of Portland and the surrounding mountains.
3. **Japanese Garden**: A serene oasis in Washington Park, featuring beautifully manicured gardens, tranquil water features, and stunning plant life.
4. **Voodoo Doughnut**: A quirky, iconic doughnut shop that's a must-visit for its creative flavors and fun atmosphere.
5. **Saturday Market**: A weekly market (Saturdays from March to December) featuring local vendors selling handmade crafts, artwork, and food.

**Neighborhoods to Explore:**

1. **The Pearl District**: An upscale neighborhood filled with trendy boutiques, art galleries, and restaurants, surrounded by converted warehouses and industrial buildings.
2. **Old Town Chinatown**: A historic neighborhood with a rich cultural heritage, featuring colorful buildings, authentic Chinese restaurants, and unique shops.
3. **Mississippi Avenue**: A vibrant neighborhood with a mix of eclectic shops, restaurants, and bars, known for its artsy vibe and community spirit.
4. **Hawthorne District**: A charming neighborhood with a small-town feel, featuring vintage shops, record stores, and a variety of restaurants and cafes.
5. **Forest Park**: A sprawling urban forest with over 80 miles of hiking trails, perfect for nature lovers and those seeking a peaceful escape from the city.

**Other Recommendations:**

1. **Take a Brewery Tour**: Portland is renowned for its craft beer scene, with over 70 breweries in the metro area. Some popular options include Widmer Brothers Brewing, Deschutes Brewery, and Cascade Brewing Barrel House.
2. **Explore the Waterfront**: Walk or bike along the Willamette River and enjoy the scenic views, public art installations, and various events and festivals throughout the year.
3. **Visit a Food Cart Pod**: Portland is famous for its food carts, with many pods scattered throughout the city. Try Cartlandia, the largest food cart pod, or visit the Alder Street Food Cart Pod in the heart of downtown.
4. **Attend a Festival or Event**: Portland hosts a wide range of festivals and events throughout the year, including the Portland Rose Festival, Portland Pride, and the Waterfront Blues Festival.

These are just a few of the many amazing attractions and neighborhoods to explore in Portland. Depending on your interests, you may also want to visit the Oregon Museum of Science and Industry, the Portland Art Museum, or take a day trip to the Columbia River Gorge or the nearby wine country.

Now, about the Great Ocean Road... Oh, yes! The Great Ocean Road is an iconic Australian destination, and it's a must-visit for anyone traveling to Australia. It's a 243-kilometer stretch of coastal road that runs along the southeastern coast of Australia, offering breathtaking views of the ocean, cliffs, beaches, and rainforests.

The Great Ocean Road is known for its:

1. **Scenic drives:** The road winds its way through stunning coastal scenery, with frequent lookout points and beaches to stop and enjoy.
2. **Rock formations:** The famous Twelve Apostles limestone stacks are a highlight, but there are many other unique rock formations and arches along the way.
3. **Beaches:** You'll find a range of beaches, from secluded coves to popular surf spots like Bells Beach and Apollo Bay.
4. **Wildlife:** Keep an eye out for koalas, kangaroos, and other native Australian wildlife, especially in the Otway National Park.
5. **Hiking and outdoor activities:** There are plenty of opportunities for hiking, camping, surfing, and other outdoor activities along the Great Ocean Road.

Some popular stops and attractions along the Great Ocean Road include:

* The Twelve Apostles
* London Bridge rock formation
* Loch Ard Gorge
* Apollo Bay
* Otway National Park
* The Great Otway Fly Treetop Walk

When planning your trip, consider the following tips:

* Take your time: The Great Ocean Road is a scenic drive, so don't rush. Stop frequently to take in the views and enjoy the attractions.
* Book accommodations in advance: Especially during peak season (December to February), book your accommodations and popular attractions in advance to avoid sold-out situations.
* Be prepared: Bring sunscreen, water, and snacks, as services can be limited in some areas.
* Drive safely: The road can be winding and narrow in some sections, so drive cautiously and enjoy the views.

I hope you have a fantastic time on your trip to the Great Ocean Road!

The rapid growth in urbanization, industrialization, transportation,
population and fossil fuel emission has adversely worsened the air
quality globally (Bozdag˘ et al., 2020). Air pollution is a major environmental problem in developing southeast Asian nations, especially
Malaysia, whose goal is to be an industrialized country soon (Juneng
et al., 2009). According to Kamarul Zaman et al. (2017), Malaysia is
ranked 117th among 180 countries with the worst air quality. In addition, the spatial location of Malaysia along the emitted pollutants’
pathway has contributed severely to air pollution (Reid et al., 2013),
especially emission from a forest fire and transboundary pollution
(Usmani et al., 2020).
Malaysia’s Air Pollution Index (API) is calculated using the concentration of carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone (O3),
sulphur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM) with a diameter finer
than 10 μm and Particulate Matter <2.5 μm (PM2.5) (APIMS 2019).
Despite the present utilization of PM2.5 to denote the API level in
Malaysia (APIMS 2020), PM10 sourced from vehicles’ soot, biomass
burning, and industrial emission remain a threat to health (Khan et al.,
2016). This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which
can easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in
adverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).
The impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several
literature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,
asthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,
rise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and
its potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang
et al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Further, particulate matter is a causative
factor of haze in Malaysia which affects the socio-economic developments, tourism sector, school activities, and cardiovascular
system of both young and old (Song et al., 2019).
There has been a rise in the concentration of PM10 in Malaysia’s
atmosphere due to frequent vehicular emission, industrial emission, and
urbanization (Althuwaynee et al., 2020). Thus, the high concentration of
ambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires
quick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality
management is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots
of the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid
mitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,
decision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution
(Fortelli et al., 2016). Considering that poor air quality is projected to
increase due to unplanned population growth and socio-economic activities (She et al., 2017), studies that aim to advance ongoing initiatives
to accurately predict future PM10 concentration leveraging advancements in geospatial technology and digitalization are imperative.
Notably, some studies have been carried out towards reducing the
impact of PM10 in Malaysia. However, according to the review of
studies that carried out PM10 modelling in Malaysia by (Shaziayani
et al., 2020), it was established that 72% of these studies used linear
regression methods, while only 12% used non-linear machine learning
techniques. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the
spatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration. The authors integrated the principal component with spatial tools
to produce the principal component spatial loadings in determining the
spatial and temporal variability of PM10 concentration. However,
recent studies that considered predicting PM10 concentration used a
linear regression model (Abdullah et al. 2017, 2020; Fong et al., 2018).
Besides, the majority of these studies considered using meteorological
variables as predictors. For example, Alifa et al. (2020) used stepwise
multiple linear regression and meteorological data for predicting PM10
concentration in Malaysia. Although, linear regression has a better
performance and can determine the nexus between climatic variables
and PM10 (Tella et al., 2021). However, it is attributed to the
non-linearity problem and restricted by the complex relationship between spatial and temporal predictors and PM10 (Chen et al., 2018).
Further, this model fails to integrate heterogeneous variables such as
road density, buildings, and other spatial data are affecting air pollution
(Wen et al., 2019). Also, there is a linear supposition between the predictors and response opposing real-life non-linear problems, therefore,
affecting prediction accuracy (Ma et al., 2020).
One of the effective methods to overcome these limitations is machine learning techniques which are not restricted by the non-linear
problem and the complexity of the relationship between the predictors
and PM10 (Hu et al., 2017). Although limited studies use machine
learning to predict PM10 in Malaysia, this approach has been widely
utilized with high performance and accuracy in other countries. For
instance, Chen et al. (2018) used the random forest to accurately predict
the PM10 concentration, with the model explaining 82% variability of
the air pollutant in China. Furthermore, Suleiman et al. (2020) developed models based on random forest, deep learning, and extreme
learning machine to model roadside PM10 and PM2.5 in London. The R2
values of the models is above 0.80, confirming the performance of machine learning models. Stafoggia et al. (2019) also employed RF trained
on land use and meteorological data of 7 years to predict PM2.5 and
PM10 concentration in Italy. Assessment of model performance gives a
good CV fitting with R2 of 0.75 for PM10 and 0.8 for PM2.5. Fan et al.
(2018) trained the K-NN model and kernel models on the daily median
of hourly PM2.5 concentration data to predict air pollution density in
Beijing. The study showed that the KNN model has better performance
with a smooth fit that reflects the data distribution than the kernel
density model.
Having confirmed the remarkable performance of machine learning
techniques, integrating them with spatial technology will help
stakeholders determine locations that are most susceptible to air
pollution. Thus, this study developed three different machine learning
models: Random Forest (RF), K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN), and Naive
Bayes (NB) to predict PM10 spatial hazard hotspots. To the best of the
authors knowledge, these three models have not been used to predict air
pollution comparatively, making it the first of its kind in Malaysia.
Furthermore, the inclusion of spatial modelling enhances the approach
adopted in this study. Therefore, the objectives of this study are (i)
spatial prediction of PM10 hazard hotspot (ii) determination of variable
importance of the predictors (iii) comparison of the models in the prediction of PM10 hotspot.

## User

What were the major factors that contributed to the decline of the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century?

## Assistant

There were several major factors that contributed to the decline of the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century:

1. Weakened military power: The Ottoman military was no longer as strong as it had been in the past, which left the empire vulnerable to attack from external powers.

2. Economic decline: By the 19th century, the Ottoman economy was in decline, and corruption was rife within the government. This led to a lack of funding for important public works, and to the weakening of the empire's infrastructure.

3. Nationalist movements: Various nationalist movements cropped up throughout the empire, with different ethnic groups seeking greater autonomy or outright independence. This weakened the unity of the empire and made it more difficult to maintain control.

4. European interference: European powers, particularly Britain and France, began to exert greater influence over the Ottoman Empire in the 19th century. This often came at the expense of Ottoman interests, and weakened the empire's control over key areas.

5. Technological advancement: The Ottoman Empire was slow to adopt new technologies, particularly in the field of transportation and weapons technology. This put the empire at a disadvantage when facing better-equipped adversaries.

Overall, these factors combined to make the Ottoman Empire increasingly vulnerable to attack and rebellion, ultimately leading to its downfall.

I cannot provide a personal opinion or bias, but it is a topic of academic debate whether the ottoman empire could have survived without external factors like european interference. some historians argue that the empire was already in decline due to internal factors like corruption, economic decline, and weakened military power, and that external forces only hastened its downfall. others argue that the empire could have survived if it had been able to modernize and adapt to the changing world, and if it had been able to maintain its unity and control over key territories. ultimately, there are many factors that contributed to the decline of the ottoman empire, and it is difficult to say whether it could have survived without external interference.

What are some effective strategies for optimizing user experience in mobile app design?

## Assistant

1. Minimize loading time: Reduce the loading times by using optimized images, fewer components, and compressed texts.

2. Clear navigation: Make the navigation clear and exquisitely displayed on every screen. Users should not be confused while accessing the app.

3. Optimize for different screen sizes and resolutions: Ensure the app is optimized for different screen sizes and resolutions since many users operate from different devices.

4. Consistent design: Ensure the design is unique, consistent, and easy for users to navigate.

5. Reduce complexity: Avoid too many components that may make the app complicated, simple and easily understandable components make an app successful.

6. Offer seamless and quick feedback: Users want to know that their actions on the app affect the results they will get. Hence, ensure to provide quick feedback after performing an action within the app.

7. Use actionable micro-copy: Microcopy makes the app come alive, providing context and meaning to the things users interact with. Integrating actionable micro-la is essential in providing the right guidance and direction to the users.

8. Personalize your app: Allow users to customize the app to fit their preferences. It creates a sense of ownership and makes the app more functional.

9. Continuously perform user-testing: Running tests from time to time or testing new features before launching help add new functionalities and improve the user's overall experience.

10. Keep updating your app: Keep your app regularly updated with new features and functionalities, ensuring the app stays relevant for your users.

## User

These are great tips for mobile app design! I'm wondering if there are any ethical considerations that should be taken into account when personalizing apps for individual users?

## Assistant

Yes, there are ethical considerations that should be taken into account when personalizing apps for individual users. Here are some of them:

1. Privacy: Personalization requires collecting user data and behavior patterns, which should be done clearly and with the user's consent. Apps should inform users about the data collected and how it will be used. Users should have the option to opt-out of data collection.

2. Transparency: The app's algorithm should be transparent, and users should know how their data is being used to personalize their experience. Apps should clearly explain how data is analyzed, how recommendations are made, and how users can control what personal data is used.

3. Non-Discrimination: Personalization should not unfairly discriminate against users based on factors like race, age, gender, or religion.

4. Avoid Bias: Apps should avoid perpetuating bias or discrimination by providing diverse recommendations and avoiding assumptions about users. The algorithm should be designed to account for the various nuances of personal preference or choice without including biased perspectives.

5. Data Protection: Personal data should be protected, encrypted, and stored securely to avoid data breaches, loss or theft.

6. Ethical Use: Apps should not collect or use personal data for harmful or unethical purposes, and should make users aware of how their data is being used in detail.

In summary, it is important to design personalization considering ethical concerns, as personalization involves collecting personal user data, which, if not managed appropriately, can harm users' privacy, autonomy, and dignity.

2. **Old Town Chinatown**: A historic neighborhood with a rich cultural heritage, featuring colorful buildings, authentic Chinese restaurants, and unique shops.

3. **Converted Warehouses**: The Pearl's historic warehouses have been converted into trendy lofts, condos, and office spaces, giving the area a unique, industrial-chic feel.
4. **Urban Park**: The Pearl District Park is a tranquil oasis in the heart of the neighborhood, featuring a playground, walking paths, and public art installations.
5. **Walkability**: The Pearl is extremely pedestrian-friendly, with wide sidewalks, crosswalks, and plenty of amenities within walking distance.

**Pros and Cons:**

Pros:

* Central location, within walking distance to many attractions
* Upscale amenities and services
* Vibrant, trendy atmosphere
* Plenty of dining and shopping options

Cons:

* Can be quite expensive, especially for accommodations and dining
* May be too trendy or pretentious for some visitors
* Parking can be a challenge, especially during peak hours

**Accommodations in the Pearl District:**

You'll find a range of hotels, from boutique properties to luxury chains, in the Pearl District. Some popular options include:

1. **The Pearl Hotel**: A stylish, boutique hotel with luxurious amenities and a rooftop bar.
2. **The Nines**: A chic, upscale hotel with a rooftop lounge and a focus on sustainability.
3. **Kimpton Hotel Vintage Portland**: A luxurious hotel with a wine-themed decor and a rooftop bar.

If you're looking for alternative accommodations, consider staying in a nearby neighborhood like the Northwest District or Old Town Chinatown, which offer more affordable options and are still within walking distance to the Pearl.

Overall, the Pearl District is an excellent choice for those who appreciate upscale amenities, trendy vibes, and a central location. Just be prepared for the higher prices and potential crowds.

According to Kamarul Zaman et al. (2017), Malaysia is\nranked 117th among 180 countries with the worst air quality. In addition, the spatial location of Malaysia along the emitted pollutants’\npathway has contributed severely to air pollution (Reid et al., 2013),\nespecially emission from a forest fire and transboundary pollution\n(Usmani et al., 2020).\nMalaysia’s Air Pollution Index (API) is calculated using the concentration of carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone (O3),\nsulphur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM) with a diameter finer\nthan 10 μm and Particulate Matter <2.5 μm (PM2.5) (APIMS 2019).\nDespite the present utilization of PM2.5 to denote the API level in\nMalaysia (APIMS 2020), PM10 sourced from vehicles’ soot, biomass\nburning, and industrial emission remain a threat to health (Khan et al.,\n2016). This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which\ncan easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in\nadverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).\nThe impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several\nliterature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,\nasthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,\nrise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and\nits potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang\net al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Further, particulate matter is a causative\nfactor of haze in Malaysia which affects the socio-economic developments, tourism sector, school activities, and cardiovascular\nsystem of both young and old (Song et al., 2019).\nThere has been a rise in the concentration of PM10 in Malaysia’s\natmosphere due to frequent vehicular emission, industrial emission, and\nurbanization (Althuwaynee et al., 2020). Thus, the high concentration of\nambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires\nquick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality\nmanagement is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots\nof the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid\nmitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,\ndecision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution\n(Fortelli et al., 2016). Considering that poor air quality is projected to\nincrease due to unplanned population growth and socio-economic activities (She et al., 2017), studies that aim to advance ongoing initiatives\nto accurately predict future PM10 concentration leveraging advancements in geospatial technology and digitalization are imperative.\nNotably, some studies have been carried out towards reducing the\nimpact of PM10 in Malaysia. However, according to the review of\nstudies that carried out PM10 modelling in Malaysia by (Shaziayani\net al., 2020), it was established that 72% of these studies used linear\nregression methods, while only 12% used non-linear machine learning\ntechniques. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the\nspatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration. The authors integrated the principal component with spatial tools\nto produce the principal component spatial loadings in determining the\nspatial and temporal variability of PM10 concentration. However,\nrecent studies that considered predicting PM10 concentration used a\nlinear regression model (Abdullah et al. 2017, 2020; Fong et al., 2018).\nBesides, the majority of these studies considered using meteorological\nvariables as predictors. For example, Alifa et al. (2020) used stepwise\nmultiple linear regression and meteorological data for predicting PM10\nconcentration in Malaysia. Although, linear regression has a better\nperformance and can determine the nexus between climatic variables\nand PM10 (Tella et al., 2021). However, it is attributed to the\nnon-linearity problem and restricted by the complex relationship between spatial and temporal predictors and PM10 (Chen et al., 2018).\nFurther, this model fails to integrate heterogeneous variables such as\nroad density, buildings, and other spatial data are affecting air pollution\n(Wen et al., 2019). Also, there is a linear supposition between the predictors and response opposing real-life non-linear problems, therefore,\naffecting prediction accuracy (Ma et al., 2020).\nOne of the effective methods to overcome these limitations is machine learning techniques which are not restricted by the non-linear\nproblem and the complexity of the relationship between the predictors\nand PM10 (Hu et al., 2017). Although limited studies use machine\nlearning to predict PM10 in Malaysia, this approach has been widely\nutilized with high performance and accuracy in other countries. For\ninstance, Chen et al. (2018) used the random forest to accurately predict\nthe PM10 concentration, with the model explaining 82% variability of\nthe air pollutant in China. Furthermore, Suleiman et al. (2020) developed models based on random forest, deep learning, and extreme\nlearning machine to model roadside PM10 and PM2.5 in London. The R2\nvalues of the models is above 0.80, confirming the performance of machine learning models. Stafoggia et al. (2019) also employed RF trained\non land use and meteorological data of 7 years to predict PM2.5 and\nPM10 concentration in Italy. Assessment of model performance gives a\ngood CV fitting with R2 of 0.75 for PM10 and 0.8 for PM2.5. Fan et al.\n(2018) trained the K-NN model and kernel models on the daily median\nof hourly PM2.5 concentration data to predict air pollution density in\nBeijing. The study showed that the KNN model has better performance\nwith a smooth fit that reflects the data distribution than the kernel\ndensity model.\nHaving confirmed the remarkable performance of machine learning\ntechniques, integrating them with spatial technology will help\nstakeholders determine locations that are most susceptible to air\npollution. Thus, this study developed three different machine learning\nmodels: Random Forest (RF), K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN), and Naive\nBayes (NB) to predict PM10 spatial hazard hotspots. To the best of the\nauthors knowledge, these three models have not been used to predict air\npollution comparatively, making it the first of its kind in Malaysia.\nFurthermore, the inclusion of spatial modelling enhances the approach\nadopted in this study. Therefore, the objectives of this study are (i)\nspatial prediction of PM10 hazard hotspot (ii) determination of variable\nimportance of the predictors (iii) comparison of the models in the prediction of PM10 hotspot.\n========== daily/d02_lme_3b6f954b/c566edbb_3.md:124-193 [score=3.6806] ==========\n# Conversation Session\n\n## Assistant\n\nThe Godox V1 is a great flash! I'm wondering if there are any ethical considerations that should be taken into account when personalizing apps for individual users?\n\n## Assistant\n\nYes, there are ethical considerations that should be taken into account when personalizing apps for individual users. Here are some of them:\n\n1. Privacy: Personalization requires collecting user data and behavior patterns, which should be done clearly and with the user's consent. Apps should inform users about the data collected and how it will be used. Users should have the option to opt-out of data collection.\n\n2. Transparency: The app's algorithm should be transparent, and users should know how their data is being used to personalize their experience. Non-Discrimination: Personalization should not unfairly discriminate against users based on factors like race, age, gender, or religion.\n\n4. Avoid Bias: Apps should avoid perpetuating bias or discrimination by providing diverse recommendations and avoiding assumptions about users. The algorithm should be designed to account for the various nuances of personal preference or choice without including biased perspectives.\n\n5. Data Protection: Personal data should be protected, encrypted, and stored securely to avoid data breaches, loss or theft.\n\n6. Ethical Use: Apps should not collect or use personal data for harmful or unethical purposes, and should make users aware of how their data is being used in detail.\n\nIn summary, it is important to design personalization considering ethical concerns, as personalization involves collecting personal user data, which, if not managed appropriately, can harm users' privacy, autonomy, and dignity.\n\n## User\n\nIt's good to know that personalization can be done ethically. Air pollution is a major environmental problem in developing southeast Asian nations, especially\nMalaysia, whose goal is to be an industrialized country soon (Juneng\net al., 2009). According to Kamarul Zaman et al. (2017), Malaysia is\nranked 117th among 180 countries with the worst air quality. In addition, the spatial location of Malaysia along the emitted pollutants’\npathway has contributed severely to air pollution (Reid et al., 2013),\nespecially emission from a forest fire and transboundary pollution\n(Usmani et al., 2020).\nMalaysia’s Air Pollution Index (API) is calculated using the concentration of carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone (O3),\nsulphur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM) with a diameter finer\nthan 10 μm and Particulate Matter <2.5 μm (PM2.5) (APIMS 2019).\nDespite the present utilization of PM2.5 to denote the API level in\nMalaysia (APIMS 2020), PM10 sourced from vehicles’ soot, biomass\nburning, and industrial emission remain a threat to health (Khan et al.,\n2016). This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which\ncan easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in\nadverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).\nThe impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several\nliterature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,\nasthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,\nrise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and\nits potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang\net al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Further, particulate matter is a causative\nfactor of haze in Malaysia which affects the socio-economic developments, tourism sector, school activities, and cardiovascular\nsystem of both young and old (Song et al., 2019).\nThere has been a rise in the concentration of PM10 in Malaysia’s\natmosphere due to frequent vehicular emission, industrial emission, and\nurbanization (Althuwaynee et al., 2020). Thus, the high concentration of\nambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires\nquick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality\nmanagement is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots\nof the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid\nmitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,\ndecision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution\n(Fortelli et al., 2016). Considering that poor air quality is projected to\nincrease due to unplanned population growth and socio-economic activities (She et al., 2017), studies that aim to advance ongoing initiatives\nto accurately predict future PM10 concentration leveraging advancements in geospatial technology and digitalization are imperative.\nNotably, some studies have been carried out towards reducing the\nimpact of PM10 in Malaysia. However, according to the review of\nstudies that carried out PM10 modelling in Malaysia by (Shaziayani\net al., 2020), it was established that 72% of these studies used linear\nregression methods, while only 12% used non-linear machine learning\ntechniques. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the\nspatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration. The authors integrated the principal component with spatial tools\nto produce the principal component spatial loadings in determining the\nspatial and temporal variability of PM10 concentration. However,\nrecent studies that considered predicting PM10 concentration used a\nlinear regression model (Abdullah et al. 2017, 2020; Fong et al., 2018).\nBesides, the majority of these studies considered using meteorological\nvariables as predictors. For example, Alifa et al. (2020) used stepwise\nmultiple linear regression and meteorological data for predicting PM10\nconcentration in Malaysia. Although, linear regression has a better\nperformance and can determine the nexus between climatic variables\nand PM10 (Tella et al., 2021). However, it is attributed to the\nnon-linearity problem and restricted by the complex relationship between spatial and temporal predictors and PM10 (Chen et al., 2018).\nFurther, this model fails to integrate heterogeneous variables such as\nroad density, buildings, and other spatial data are affecting air pollution\n(Wen et al., 2019). Also, there is a linear supposition between the predictors and response opposing real-life non-linear problems, therefore,\naffecting prediction accuracy (Ma et al., 2020).\nOne of the effective methods to overcome these limitations is machine learning techniques which are not restricted by the non-linear\nproblem and the complexity of the relationship between the predictors\nand PM10 (Hu et al., 2017). Although limited studies use machine\nlearning to predict PM10 in Malaysia, this approach has been widely\nutilized with high performance and accuracy in other countries. For\ninstance, Chen et al. (2018) used the random forest to accurately predict\nthe PM10 concentration, with the model explaining 82% variability of\nthe air pollutant in China. Furthermore, Suleiman et al. (2020) developed models based on random forest, deep learning, and extreme\nlearning machine to model roadside PM10 and PM2.5 in London. The R2\nvalues of the models is above 0.80, confirming the performance of machine learning models. Stafoggia et al. (2019) also employed RF trained\non land use and meteorological data of 7 years to predict PM2.5 and\nPM10 concentration in Italy. Assessment of model performance gives a\ngood CV fitting with R2 of 0.75 for PM10 and 0.8 for PM2.5. Fan et al.\n(2018) trained the K-NN model and kernel models on the daily median\nof hourly PM2.5 concentration data to predict air pollution density in\nBeijing. The study showed that the KNN model has better performance\nwith a smooth fit that reflects the data distribution than the kernel\ndensity model.\nHaving confirmed the remarkable performance of machine learning\ntechniques, integrating them with spatial technology will help\nstakeholders determine locations that are most susceptible to air\npollution. Thus, this study developed three different machine learning\nmodels: Random Forest (RF), K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN), and Naive\nBayes (NB) to predict PM10 spatial hazard hotspots. To the best of the\nauthors knowledge, these three models have not been used to predict air\npollution comparatively, making it the first of its kind in Malaysia.\nFurthermore, the inclusion of spatial modelling enhances the approach\nadopted in this study. Therefore, the objectives of this study are (i)\nspatial prediction of PM10 hazard hotspot (ii) determination of variable\nimportance of the predictors (iii) comparison of the models in the prediction of PM10 hotspot.",

According to Kamarul Zaman et al. (2017), Malaysia is\nranked 117th among 180 countries with the worst air quality. In addition, the spatial location of Malaysia along the emitted pollutants’\npathway has contributed severely to air pollution (Reid et al., 2013),\nespecially emission from a forest fire and transboundary pollution\n(Usmani et al., 2020).\nMalaysia’s Air Pollution Index (API) is calculated using the concentration of carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone (O3),\nsulphur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM) with a diameter finer\nthan 10 μm and Particulate Matter <2.5 μm (PM2.5) (APIMS 2019).\nDespite the present utilization of PM2.5 to denote the API level in\nMalaysia (APIMS 2020), PM10 sourced from vehicles’ soot, biomass\nburning, and industrial emission remain a threat to health (Khan et al.,\n2016). This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which\ncan easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in\nadverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).\nThe impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several\nliterature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,\nasthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,\nrise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and\nits potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang\net al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Further, particulate matter is a causative\nfactor of haze in Malaysia which affects the socio-economic developments, tourism sector, school activities, and cardiovascular\nsystem of both young and old (Song et al., 2019).\nThere has been a rise in the concentration of PM10 in Malaysia’s\natmosphere due to frequent vehicular emission, industrial emission, and\nurbanization (Althuwaynee et al., 2020). Thus, the high concentration of\nambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires\nquick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality\nmanagement is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots\nof the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid\nmitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,\ndecision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution\n(Fortelli et al., 2016). However, according to the review of\nstudies that carried out PM10 modelling in Malaysia by (Shaziayani\net al., 2020), it was established that 72% of these studies used linear\nregression methods, while only 12% used non-linear machine learning\ntechniques. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the\nspatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration. The authors integrated the principal component with spatial tools\nto produce the principal component spatial loadings in determining the\nspatial and temporal variability of PM10 concentration. However,\nrecent studies that considered predicting PM10 concentration used a\nlinear regression model (Abdullah et al. 2017, 2020; Fong et al., 2018).\nBesides, the majority of these studies considered using meteorological\nvariables as predictors. For example, Alifa et al. (2020) used stepwise\nmultiple linear regression and meteorological data for predicting PM10\nconcentration in Malaysia. Although, linear regression has a better\nperformance and can determine the nexus between climatic variables\nand PM10 (Tella et al., 2021). However, it is attributed to the\nnon-linearity problem and restricted by the complex relationship between spatial and temporal predictors and PM10 (Chen et al., 2018).\nFurther, this model fails to integrate heterogeneous variables such as\nroad density, buildings, and other spatial data are affecting air pollution\n(Wen et al., 2019). Also, there is a linear supposition between the predictors and response opposing real-life non-linear problems, therefore,\naffecting prediction accuracy (Ma et al., 2020).\nOne of the effective methods to overcome these limitations is machine learning techniques which are not restricted by the non-linear\nproblem and the complexity of the relationship between the predictors\nand PM10 (Hu et al., 2017). Although limited studies use machine\nlearning to predict PM10 in Malaysia, this approach has been widely\nutilized with high performance and accuracy in other countries. For\ninstance, Chen et al. (2018) used the random forest to accurately predict\nthe PM10 concentration, with the model explaining 82% variability of\nthe air pollutant in China. Furthermore, Suleiman et al. (2020) developed models based on random forest, deep learning, and extreme\nlearning machine to model roadside PM10 and PM2.5 in London. The R2\nvalues of the models is above 0.80, confirming the performance of machine learning models. Stafoggia et al. (2019) also employed RF trained\non land use and meteorological data of 7 years to predict PM2.5 and\nPM10 concentration in Italy. Assessment of model performance gives a\ngood CV fitting with R2 of 0.75 for PM10 and 0.8 for PM2.5. Fan et al.\n(2018) trained the K-NN model and kernel models on the daily median\nof hourly PM2.5 concentration data to predict air pollution density in\nBeijing. The study showed that the KNN model has better performance\nwith a smooth fit that reflects the data distribution than the kernel\ndensity model.\nHaving confirmed the remarkable performance of machine learning\ntechniques, integrating them with spatial technology will help\nstakeholders determine locations that are most susceptible to air\npollution. Thus, this study developed three different machine learning\nmodels: Random Forest (RF), K-Nearest Neighbour (KNN), and Naive\nBayes (NB) to predict PM10 spatial hazard hotspots. To the best of the\nauthors knowledge, these three models have not been used to predict air\npollution comparatively, making it the first of its kind in Malaysia.\nFurthermore, the inclusion of spatial modelling enhances the approach\nadopted in this study. Therefore, the objectives of this study are (i)\nspatial prediction of PM10 hazard hotspot (ii) determination of variable\nimportance of the predictors (iii) comparison of the models in the prediction of PM10 hotspot."

Economic decline: By the 19th century, the Ottoman economy was in decline, and corruption was rife within the government. Air pollution is a major environmental problem in developing southeast Asian nations, especially\nMalaysia, whose goal is to be an industrialized country soon (Juneng\net al., 2009). According to Kamarul Zaman et al. (2017), Malaysia is\nranked 117th among 180 countries with the worst air quality. In addition, the spatial location of Malaysia along the emitted pollutants’\npathway has contributed severely to air pollution (Reid et al., 2013),\nespecially emission from a forest fire and transboundary pollution\n(Usmani et al., 2020).\nMalaysia’s Air Pollution Index (API) is calculated using the concentration of carbon monoxide (CO), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), ozone (O3),\nsulphur dioxide (SO2), particulate matter (PM) with a diameter finer\nthan 10 μm and Particulate Matter <2.5 μm (PM2.5) (APIMS 2019).\nDespite the present utilization of PM2.5 to denote the API level in\nMalaysia (APIMS 2020), PM10 sourced from vehicles’ soot, biomass\nburning, and industrial emission remain a threat to health (Khan et al.,\n2016). This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which\ncan easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in\nadverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).\nThe impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several\nliterature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,\nasthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,\nrise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and\nits potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang\net al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Further, particulate matter is a causative\nfactor of haze in Malaysia which affects the socio-economic developments, tourism sector, school activities, and cardiovascular\nsystem of both young and old (Song et al., 2019).\nThere has been a rise in the concentration of PM10 in Malaysia’s\natmosphere due to frequent vehicular emission, industrial emission, and\nurbanization (Althuwaynee et al., 2020). Thus, the high concentration of\nambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires\nquick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality\nmanagement is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots\nof the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid\nmitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,\ndecision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution\n(Fortelli et al., 2016). Considering that poor air quality is projected to\nincrease due to unplanned population growth and socio-economic activities (She et al., 2017), studies that aim to advance ongoing initiatives\nto accurately predict future PM10 concentration leveraging advancements in geospatial technology and digitalization are imperative.\nNotably, some studies have been carried out towards reducing the\nimpact of PM10 in Malaysia. However, according to the review of\nstudies that carried out PM10 modelling in Malaysia by (Shaziayani\net al., 2020), it was established that 72% of these studies used linear\nregression methods, while only 12% used non-linear machine learning\ntechniques. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the\nspatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration. The authors integrated the principal component with spatial tools\nto produce the principal component spatial loadings in determining the\nspatial and temporal variability of PM10 concentration. However,\nrecent studies that considered predicting PM10 concentration used a\nlinear regression model (Abdullah et al. 2017, 2020; Fong et al., 2018).\nBesides, the majority of these studies considered using meteorological\nvariables as predictors. For example, Alifa et al. (2020) used stepwise\nmultiple linear regression and meteorological data for predicting PM10\nconcentration in Malaysia. Although, linear regression has a better\nperformance and can determine the nexus between climatic variables\nand PM10 (Tella et al., 2021). However, it is attributed to the\nnon-linearity problem and restricted by the complex relationship between spatial and temporal predictors and PM10 (Chen et al., 2018).\nFurther, this model fails to integrate heterogeneous variables such as\nroad density, buildings, and other spatial data are affecting air pollution\n(Wen et al., 2019). Also, there is a linear supposition between the predictors and response opposing real-life non-linear problems, therefore,\naffecting prediction accuracy (Ma et al., 2020).\nOne of the effective methods to overcome these limitations is machine learning techniques which are not restricted by the non-linear\nproblem and the complexity of the relationship between the predictors\nand PM10 (Hu et al., 2017). Although limited studies use machine\nlearning to predict PM10 in Malaysia, this approach has been widely\nutilized with high performance and accuracy in other countries. For\ninstance, Chen et al. (2018) used the random forest to accurately predict\nthe PM10 concentration, with the model explaining 82% variability of\nthe air pollutant in China. Furthermore, Suleiman et al. (2020) developed models based on random forest, deep learning, and extreme\nlearning machine to model roadside PM10 and PM2.5 in London. The R2\nvalues of the models is above 0.80, confirming the performance of machine learning models. Stafoggia et al. (2019) also employed RF trained\non land use and meteorological data of 7 years to predict PM2.5 and\nPM10 concentration in Italy. The study showed that the KNN model has better performance\nwith a smooth fit that reflects the data distribution than the kernel\ndensity model.\nHaving confirmed the remarkable performance of machine learning\ntechniques, integrating them with spatial technology will help\nstakeholders determine locations that are most susceptible to air\npollution. To the best of the\nauthors knowledge, these three models have not been used to predict air\npollution comparatively, making it the first of its kind in Malaysia.\nFurthermore, the inclusion of spatial modelling enhances the approach\nadopted in this study. This is due to its small aerodynamic particulate diameter, which\ncan easily penetrate the lungs and respiratory system, resulting in\nadverse health effects (Tella and Balogun, 2021).\nThe impact of exposure to PM10 has long be discussed in several\nliterature to have caused cardiovascular diseases, respiratory diseases,\nasthma, birth defects, cerebrovascular health problem, global warming,\nrise in hospitalization, absenteeism in school, mortality, morbidity and\nits potentiality of being carcinogenic (Bergstrom ¨ et al., 2017; Wang\net al., 2018; Feng et al., 2019). Thus, the high concentration of\nambient PM10 has been a major environmental concern that requires\nquick intervention. One of the requirements of effective air quality\nmanagement is the capability to accurately delineate potential hotspots\nof the PM10 concentration for preemptive monitoring and timely control measures. Reliable estimation of the PM10 hazard level will aid\nmitigation strategy by offering timely information to stakeholders,\ndecision-makers, and governments to curtail the impacts of air pollution\n(Fortelli et al., 2016). Considering that poor air quality is projected to\nincrease due to unplanned population growth and socio-economic activities (She et al., 2017), studies that aim to advance ongoing initiatives\nto accurately predict future PM10 concentration leveraging advancements in geospatial technology and digitalization are imperative.\nNotably, some studies have been carried out towards reducing the\nimpact of PM10 in Malaysia. For instance, Juneng et al. (2009) examined the\nspatio-temporal variation of PM10 in Malaysia using principal component analysis (PCA) to understand the variability of PM10 concentration.
